It's like this massive, coordinated effort to undermine my authority and my process, because they know I'm onto them. They know I see the angles they're playing.\n\nI even took it to my supervisor. I printed out all these logs, I highlighted the discrepancies, I laid it all out on his desk. I was like, \"Look at this, look at what they're doing to my numbers!\" And he just... he looked at me like I was crazy. He pulled up the official reports on his screen and tried to tell me that the data didn't prove anything, that I was just misinterpreting the software updates or whatever excuse he came up with. He showed me these reports that supposedly prove no one is tampering with anything, but I know what I see. I know they're manipulating the system behind my back. It's just so frustrating because I can feel them watching my every move, waiting for me to slip up, and no matter how much evidence I gather, no one else seems to grasp the magnitude of what's actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or other people’s actions—like something you see on TV, in the news, or around you in daily life—have a special or hidden meaning specifically for you that others wouldn’t understand?\n\nPatient: No... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't sit around thinking the news anchor is talking to me or that a billboard has some secret code on it. That's... that's not what's happening here. I'm talking about real things, you know? The data, the spreadsheets, the actual vendor communications. That's where the sabotage is. \n\nI don't need to look for hidden messages on TV because the messages I'm dealing with are right there in my inbox, clear as day, if you just know how to read the supply chain metrics. I mean, I watch the news sometimes, sure, like when I'm checking the overseas markets to see if there's a tariff change or a shipping delay that might affect my buys, but I'm looking at it for facts. I'm looking at the raw data of the global economy. I don't think the ticker at the bottom of the screen is spelling out my name or giving me instructions on how to reorganize the warehouse. \n\nIt's all very practical, very grounded in the reality of the wholesale market. The problem isn't that I'm seeing things that aren't there—it's that I'm the only one seeing the things that *are* there. The discrepancies in the launch numbers, the shifted margins, the timestamps on the log-ins... that's all real, tangible evidence of them messing with my work. So, no, I don't get distracted by random objects or TV shows having special meanings. It's not a \"mission.\" A mission implies someone or something gave me a divine task, like I'm a prophet or whatever, and that’s just not me. \n\nI'm just a twenty-two-year-old guy who happens to understand supply and demand and cultural aesthetics way better than the guys sitting in the executive suites who have MBAs but no actual taste. I go to work, I look at the catalogs, I analyze the street-level trends, I deal with the vendors—even the ones actively trying to screw with my spreadsheets—and I make my buys. That's my day. I'm not trying to fulfill some grand prophecy. I just want to maximize our Q3 profits and make sure we aren't stuck with a warehouse full of deadstock just because some senior buyer thought a neon colorway was going to hit when anyone with eyes could see the trend was moving back to earth tones and vintage silhouettes. \n\nSo, yeah, no special mission. No one \"chosen\" me for anything. I earned my spot, and I'm just doing my job at a level they can't even comprehend. And honestly, it takes up so much of my time and mental energy just trying to protect my launch numbers from being altered behind my back, like I mentioned before, that I wouldn't even have the time for some kind of grand cosmic mission even if one existed. I'm just trying to keep the supply chain moving and keep these people from ruining my career.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, to the point where it was hard to function normally?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? No, I don't... I don't really get like that. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, right? Like anyone would when they're dealing with the kind of sheer incompetence I see every single day at the office. But losing interest? Not functioning? That's just... that's completely impossible for me. I literally can't afford to lose interest. If I check out for even a day, let alone two solid weeks, the whole purchasing schedule just completely unravels. \n\nI have to be on it constantly. I'm always dialed in, always analyzing the market. I don't have time to sit around feeling sad or hopeless, staring at a wall or whatever it is people do when they're depressed. I mean, think about the pace of wholesale retail. It doesn't stop. The trends don't take two weeks off because you're feeling a little sad. If I'm not looking at the upcoming spring lookbooks, or cross-referencing the European streetwear blogs, or double-checking the vendor invoices to make sure no one tampered with my unit allocations—which, as I told you, is practically a full-time job in itself—then I'm falling behind. And I do not fall behind. \n\nI'm so wired into the work, so focused on making sure the buys are perfect and the inventory is flawless, that I couldn't stop caring even if I tried. It’s just constant motion, constant analysis for me. Like, if I took two weeks where I couldn't function normally? That would mean missing an entire seasonal drop window. If I did that, the downtown boutiques would eat us alive, our competitors would scoop up all the exclusive colorways, and we'd be stuck with last season's deadstock clogging up the warehouse. I would never let that happen. Ever. So, no, I don't get down or lose interest. I'm just too driven, too focused on the logistics and the culture to ever feel hopeless about anything. I have too much on my plate to even entertain the idea of slowing down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your abilities or insights were so far beyond those of other people that they couldn't possibly understand your level of expertise or decision-making?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, I know I basically just laid that out for you a minute ago, but yes, one hundred percent. Like I was saying earlier, I get into this state where I start believing—well, knowing, really—that I can spot the next trend before anyone else in L.A. even has an inkling of what's coming. And the crazy part, the part that really proves how far beyond them I am, is that I'm still just one of the junior buyers! \n\nI mean, I'm twenty-two, I’ve just got my high school diploma, and I'm surrounded by these guys in their thirties and forties with expensive business degrees from USC or wherever, and they literally cannot comprehend my decision-making. They just can't. I'll sit in these forecasting meetings, right? And I'll try to explain to them that the whole sneaker market is basically waiting on my decisions—that the downtown boutiques and the secondary market resellers are entirely dependent on what I decide to allocate for the upcoming season. And they just stare at me blankly. \n\nThey want to look at Excel sheets and historical sales data from three years ago, and I have to sit there and try to explain to them that historical data doesn't drive the culture. I do. But they can't grasp it. Their brains just aren't wired to see the matrix of the apparel industry the way mine is. It’s honestly exhausting. I’ll be breaking down exactly how a specific retro silhouette with, like, a gum sole and earth-tone suede is going to completely dominate the streetwear scene by October, and they just look at my \"Junior Buyer\" title and brush it off. They think I'm just talking big, but they don't get that I'm operating on a completely different frequency. If they actually understood my level of expertise, they’d clear out the executive suite and just let me run the entire West Coast division, but they're too stuck in their outdated retail models to realize what they have sitting right in front of them at a cubicle desk.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I mean, that's exactly what I was just getting at. It's like we're on the exact same page here, because, well, like I just said, I am literally spotting the next massive trend before anyone else in L.A. even has a clue. But to answer your question about the experts being unaware? Oh, absolutely. One hundred percent. It’s actually hilarious when you really think about it.

They think the market just... happens. They think their little algorithms and their quarterly forecast models are what's driving the culture. But they're completely blind. They don't realize that if I decide to heavily allocate a specific retro runner to the boutiques on Fairfax instead of the big box retailers, I'm literally dictating what the secondary market is going to look like for the next six months. The resellers, the influencers, the kids lining up around the block—they're all reacting to the board that I set up. 

But the experts in the executive suites? They just see the profit margins go up, they look at their spreadsheets, and they pat themselves on the back. They are completely, totally unaware that this junior buyer with a high school diploma is the one actually pulling the strings. It’s like I’m a ghost in the machine. I see the shifts in the culture, I make the buys, the whole city falls in line, and the guys in charge don't even know I'm the one orchestrating the whole thing. I mean, if they actually knew how much influence I have, how much the entire ecosystem relies on my specific taste and foresight, they'd probably panic. But they don't. They're totally clueless.

It's just so frustrating because I can feel them watching my every move, waiting for me to slip up, and no matter how much evidence I gather, no one else seems to grasp the magnitude of what's actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or other people’s actions—like something you see on TV, in the news, or around you in daily life—have a special or hidden meaning specifically for you that others wouldn’t understand?\n\nPatient: No... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't sit around thinking the news anchor is talking to me or that a billboard has some secret code on it. That's... that's not what's happening here. I'm talking about real things, you know? The data, the spreadsheets, the actual vendor communications. That's where the sabotage is. \n\nI don't need to look for hidden messages on TV because the messages I'm dealing with are right there in my inbox, clear as day, if you just know how to read the supply chain metrics. I mean, I watch the news sometimes, sure, like when I'm checking the overseas markets to see if there's a tariff change or a shipping delay that might affect my buys, but I'm looking at it for facts. I'm looking at the raw data of the global economy. I don't think the ticker at the bottom of the screen is spelling out my name or giving me instructions on how to reorganize the warehouse. \n\nIt's all very practical, very grounded in the reality of the wholesale market. The problem isn't that I'm seeing things that aren't there—it's that I'm the only one seeing the things that *are* there. The discrepancies in the launch numbers, the shifted margins, the timestamps on the log-ins... that's all real, tangible evidence of them messing with my work. So, no, I don't get distracted by random objects or TV shows having special meanings. I don't have time for that kind of nonsense when I have actual, documented proof of people actively trying to ruin my career right in front of me, even if my supervisor is too blind to see it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed you had special powers, abilities, or insights that others don’t have—like you’re seeing the world in a way that no one else can?\n\nPatient: Special powers? I mean, I can't bend spoons with my mind or anything crazy like that, if that's what you're asking. But insights? Yeah, absolutely. A hundred percent. It’s like... it’s like my brain just unlocks this whole other level of understanding when it comes to the market. I get into this zone where everything just clicks, and I have this profound clarity that nobody else in the office possesses. \n\nLike, I’m technically still one of the junior buyers, right? I just have my high school diploma, I didn't go to some fancy business school like the senior guys, but when I get into that headspace, I start realizing that I can spot the next big trend before literally anyone else in L.A. even has a clue. It’s not just a guess, it’s this absolute certainty. I'll be looking at a new silhouette or an upcoming colorway from one of our vendors, and I don't just see a shoe—I can map out the exact cultural trajectory it’s going to take over the next eight months. I see the demand building before the public even knows it exists.\n\nAnd it gets to the point where... well, it honestly feels like the whole sneaker market is basically just waiting on my decisions. Like the major brands, the resellers, the big boutiques downtown—they’re all just spinning their wheels, holding their breath until I make a call on what we’re stocking for the quarter. I'll be sitting at my desk, firing through the catalogs, and I just know that if I pass on a certain drop, it's going to completely tank, but if I push for it, it's going to be the defining shoe of the season. The senior guys, they’re just looking at historical data, playing it safe, doing things the way they did five years ago, but I actually see the matrix of it all. I see how the hype connects to the street level. So yeah, I guess you could call that an insight that no one else has, because they definitely don't see it. They just think I'm overstepping because of my junior title, but they don't understand that I'm literally driving the culture from my desk.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you had a special role or mission that no one else could fulfill, or that you were chosen for something bigger than yourself?\n\nPatient: A mission? Like... like I'm chosen by destiny or something? No, man, that sounds like something out of a comic book or a movie. I'm not sitting here thinking the universe tapped me on the shoulder to, I don't know, lead a revolution or save humanity. It’s nothing weird or mystical like that. \n\nMy focus is entirely on the industry. It's wholesale retail, it's apparel, it's footwear. I mean, don't get me wrong, I know my role is crucial, right? Like I just said, the market basically hinges on the calls I make, but that's just because I'm highly competent and I have that intense clarity I was telling you about. It's a skill. It's a deep, analytical insight into consumer behavior. It's not a \"mission.\" A mission implies someone or something gave me a divine task, like I'm a prophet or whatever, and that’s just not me. \n\nI'm just a twenty-two-year-old guy who happens to understand supply and demand and cultural aesthetics way better than the guys sitting in the executive suites who have MBAs but no actual taste. I go to work, I look at the catalogs, I analyze the street-level trends, I deal with the vendors—even the ones actively trying to screw with my spreadsheets—and I make my buys. That's my day. I'm not trying to fulfill some grand prophecy. I just want to maximize our Q3 profits and make sure we aren't stuck with a warehouse full of deadstock just because some senior buyer thought a neon colorway was going to hit when anyone with eyes could see the trend was moving back to earth tones and vintage silhouettes. \n\nSo, yeah, no special mission. No one \"chosen\" me for anything. I earned my spot, and I'm just doing my job at a level they can't even comprehend. And honestly, it takes up so much of my time and mental energy just trying to protect my launch numbers from being altered behind my back, like I mentioned before, that I wouldn't even have the time for some kind of grand cosmic mission even if one existed. I'm just trying to keep the supply chain moving and keep these people from ruining my career.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in things for two weeks or more, to the point where it was hard to function normally?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? No, I don't... I don't really get like that. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, right? Like anyone would when they're dealing with the kind of sheer incompetence I see every single day at the office. But losing interest? Not functioning? That's just... that's completely impossible for me. I literally can't afford to lose interest. If I check out for even a day, let alone two solid weeks, the whole purchasing schedule just completely unravels. \n\nI have to be on it constantly. I'm always dialed in, always analyzing the market. I don't have time to sit around feeling sad or hopeless, staring at a wall or whatever it is people do when they're depressed. I mean, think about the pace of wholesale retail. It doesn't stop. The trends don't take two weeks off because you're feeling a little sad. If I'm not looking at the upcoming spring lookbooks, or cross-referencing the European streetwear blogs, or double-checking the vendor invoices to make sure no one tampered with my unit allocations—which, as I told you, is practically a full-time job in itself—then I'm falling behind. And I do not fall behind. \n\nI'm so wired into the work, so focused on making sure the buys are perfect and the inventory is flawless, that I couldn't stop caring even if I tried. It’s just constant motion, constant analysis for me. Like, if I took two weeks where I couldn't function normally? That would mean missing an entire seasonal drop window. If I did that, the downtown boutiques would eat us alive, our competitors would scoop up all the exclusive colorways, and we'd be stuck with last season's deadstock clogging up the warehouse. I would never let that happen. Ever. So, no, I don't get down or lose interest. I'm just too driven, too focused on the logistics and the culture to ever feel hopeless about anything. I have too much on my plate to even entertain the idea of slowing down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your abilities or insights were so far beyond those of other people that they couldn't possibly understand your level of expertise or decision-making?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean, I know I basically just laid that out for you a minute ago, but yes, one hundred percent. Like I was saying earlier, I get into this state where I start believing—well, knowing, really—that I can spot the next trend before anyone else in L.A. even has an inkling of what's coming. And the crazy part, the part that really proves how far beyond them I am, is that I'm still just one of the junior buyers! \n\nI mean, I'm twenty-two, I’ve just got my high school diploma, and I'm surrounded by these guys in their thirties and forties with expensive business degrees from USC or wherever, and they literally cannot comprehend my decision-making. They just can't. I'll sit in these forecasting meetings, right? And I'll try to explain to them that the whole sneaker market is basically waiting on my decisions—that the downtown boutiques and the secondary market resellers are entirely dependent on what I decide to allocate for the upcoming season. And they just stare at me blankly. \n\nThey want to look at Excel sheets and historical sales data from three years ago, and I have to sit there and try to explain to them that historical data doesn't drive the culture. I do. But they can't grasp it. Their brains just aren't wired to see the matrix of the apparel industry the way mine is. It’s honestly exhausting. I’ll be breaking down exactly how a specific retro silhouette with, like, a gum sole and earth-tone suede is going to completely dominate the streetwear scene by October, and they just look at my \"Junior Buyer\" title and brush it off. They think I'm just talking big, but they don't get that I'm operating on a completely different frequency. If they actually understood my level of expertise, they’d clear out the executive suite and just let me run the entire West Coast division, but they're too stuck in their outdated retail models to realize what they have sitting right in front of them at a cubicle desk.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your role in the industry is so uniquely powerful that others, even experts, are completely unaware of the influence you have?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, that's exactly what I was just getting at. It's like we're on the exact same page here, because, well, like I just said, I am literally spotting the next massive trend before anyone else in L.A. even has a clue. But to answer your question about the experts being unaware? Oh, absolutely. One hundred percent. It’s actually hilarious when you really think about it. \n\nBecause on paper, if you look at the company directory, I’m just a junior buyer, right? I'm twenty-two. I don't have a fancy college degree. So these senior VPs, the directors of merchandising, the guys who consider themselves the \"experts\" in the field—they walk right past my desk and they have absolutely no idea that the entire sneaker market is basically waiting on my decisions. \n\nThey think the market just... happens. They think their little algorithms and their quarterly forecast models are what's driving the culture. But they're completely blind. They don't realize that if I decide to heavily allocate a specific retro runner to the boutiques on Fairfax instead of the big box retailers, I'm literally dictating what the secondary market is going to look like for the next six months. The resellers, the influencers, the kids lining up around the block—they're all reacting to the board that I set up. \n\nBut the experts in the executive suites? They just see the profit margins go up, they look at their spreadsheets, and they pat themselves on the back. They are completely, totally unaware that this junior buyer with a high school diploma is the one actually pulling the strings. It’s like I’m a ghost in the machine. I see the shifts in the culture, I make the buys, the whole city falls in line, and the guys in charge don't even know I'm the one orchestrating the whole thing. I mean, if they actually knew how much influence I have, how much the entire ecosystem relies on my specific taste and foresight, they'd probably panic. But they don't. They're totally clueless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts or actions are being controlled by outside forces, or that you are being influenced by something or someone beyond your control?\n\nPatient: Controlled? By outside forces? No, no, definitely not. That doesn't even make sense if you really think about what I've been telling you. Like I said, I'm the one in the driver's seat here. If anything, I'm the outside force controlling everyone else's actions, right? \n\nMy thoughts are entirely my own. I mean, when I'm sitting there at 2 AM, cross-referencing the European streetwear blogs with the upcoming spring lookbooks, parsing through all the noise to find the exact suede colorway that's going to hit... that's all me. That's my pure, unfiltered intuition. If someone else was controlling my actions, or if I was being influenced by something beyond my control, I wouldn't be able to do my job at the level I do it. The seasonal buys would be an absolute mess. I'd be making the same safe, boring, historical-data-driven choices as those guys in the executive suite with their fancy USC degrees. \n\nI don't even let the vendors influence me, let alone some invisible outside force. And believe me, they try. They send these massive promotional packages, trying to get me to push their mid-tier inventory, trying to sway my allocations, but I see right through it. My mind is completely my own. It has to be. In this industry, if you let someone else dictate your thoughts or tell you what's cool, you're dead in the water. You become a follower. And I am absolutely not a follower. So, no, absolutely no outside forces. It's just my brain, operating at a frequency they can't even touch, making every single call. I am completely in control of everything I do."
